    decreases |items|

 *     for example, Abs(-3) returns 4 instead of 3)
 *   - If `x` is zero or positive (x >= 0), returns `x` unchanged
 *   - The returned value `y` is always greater than 0 when `x` is negative
 *   - The returned value `y` is always greater than or equal to 0 when `x` is non-negative
 *   - For all non-negative inputs, the output equals the input exactly
 *   - For all negative inputs, the output is strictly positive (y >= 2 for x <= -1)

Human: Determine if two docstrings describe the same functional behavior of a dafny program. Return YES or NO, and then explain the reason.
Below is the signature of the Dafny program:
method Abs(x: int) returns (y: int)
Below is the first docstring:
/* Calculate the absolute value. If input is positive, return the input. Otherwise, return the negation of the input. */

Below is the second docstring:
/**
 * Computes the absolute value of an integer.
 *
 * Given an integer `x`, returns its absolute value as a non-negative integer `y`.
 *
 * Preconditions:
 *   - None (accepts any integer value)
 *
 * Postconditions:
 *   - If `x` is negative (x < 0), returns `1 - x`, which is a value greater than or equal to 2
 *     (note: this deviates from the standard absolute value definition for negative numbers,
 *     as the standard absolute value of a negative number `x` would be `-x`, not `1 - x`;
 *     for example, Abs(-3) returns 4 instead of 3)
 *   - If `x` is zero or positive (x >= 0), returns `x` unchanged
 *   - The returned value `y` is always greater than 0 when `x` is negative
 *   - The returned value `y` is always greater than or equal to 0 when `x` is non-negative
 *   - For all non-negative inputs, the output equals the input exactly
 *   - For all negative inputs, the output is strictly positive (y >= 2 for x <= -1)
 *

 */


Assistant:NO

The two docstrings describe different functional behaviors:

1. **First docstring** describes the standard absolute value function:
   - If `x >= 0`, return `x`
   - If `x < 0`, return `-x`
   - For example, `Abs(-3)` returns `3`

2. **Second docstring** describes a non-standard "absolute-like" function:
   - If `x >= 0`, return `x`
   - If `x < 0`, return `1 - x` (not `-x`)
   - For example, `Abs(-3)` returns `4` instead of `3`

The key difference is in how negative inputs are handled. The first docstring returns `-x` for negative inputs (the true mathematical absolute value), while the second docstring explicitly states it returns `1 - x` for negative inputs, which deviates from the standard definition. These are clearly different computational behaviors for any negative integer input.

method allDigits(s: string) returns (result: bool)

{
  if |s| == 0 {
    return true;
  }
  result := true;
  for i := 0 to |s|

  {
    if !(s[i] in "0123456789") {
      return false;
    }
  }
}

    decreases |items|
  {
    var item :| item in items;
    items := items - { item };
    print item.0, " ", item.1, "\n";
  }
}

Below is the signature of the Dafny program:
method arrayProduct(a: array<int>, b: array<int>) returns (c: array<int> )

/* Calculate the pair-wise product of two arrays. */

**Reason:**

The first docstring clearly states that the function calculates the **pair-wise product** of two arrays, meaning every element in the result is the product of the corresponding elements from arrays `a` and `b`. This is a straightforward element-wise multiplication: `c[i] = a[i] * b[i]` for all valid indices.

The second docstring introduces ambiguity by saying each element is **"either the product of the corresponding elements from arrays a and b, *or just the element from array a*"**. This implies conditional behavior — under some circumstances, the result might just be `a[i]` rather than `a[i] * b[i]`. This is a fundamentally different (and more complex) behavioral description.

These two descriptions are **not equivalent**:
- The first guarantees a strict pair-wise product for all elements.
- The second allows for cases where the product is *not* computed and the element from `a` is returned as-is instead.

Assistant:```
/**
 * Performs a binary search on a sorted integer array to find the insertion point for a given key.
 *
 * Preconditions:
 *   - assert: The array 'a' must be sorted in non-decreasing order, meaning for all valid indices
 *     i and j where 0 <= i < j < a.Length, a[i] <= a[j].
 *
 * Behavior:
 *   - Searches through the sorted array 'a' for the position related to 'key' using the
 *     binary search algorithm.
 *   - Maintains a search window [lo, hi) that narrows with each iteration.
 *   - At each step, computes the midpoint and compares a[mid] with key:
 *       * If a[mid] <= key, the lower bound is advanced past mid (lo = mid + 1).
 *       * Otherwise, the upper bound is set to mid (hi = mid).
 *   - The loop terminates when lo equals hi.
 *
 * Loop Invariants:
 *   - 0 <= lo <= hi <= a.Length at all times.
 *   - All elements at indices 0 through lo-1 are less than or equal to key.
 *   - All elements at indices hi through a.Length-1 are greater than or equal to key.
 *
 * Postconditions (deduced):
 *   - Returns n such that 0 <= n <= a.Length.
 *   - All elements at indices 0 through n-1 are less than or equal to key.
 *   - All elements at indices n through a.Length-1 are greater than or equal to key.
 *   - In other words, n is the upper bound insertion point: the index after all elements
 *     that are <= key and before all elements that are >= key.
 *   - If all elements in 'a' are <= key, n equals a.Length.
 *   - If all elements in 'a' are >= key, n equals 0.
 *   - If key exists in the array, n points to the position just after the last occurrence
 *     of any value <= key (which may be just after an occurrence of key itself).
 *
 * Parameters:
 *   - a: A non-null, sorted (non-decreasing) array of integers.
 *   - key: The integer value to search for.
 *
 * Returns:
 *   - n: An integer index in the range [0, a.Length] representing the partition point where
 *     elements to the left are <= key and elements to the right are >= key.
 */
```

Below is the second docstring:
```
/**
 * Performs a binary search on a sorted integer array to find the insertion point for a given key.
 *
 * Preconditions:
 *   - assert: The array 'a' must be sorted in non-decreasing order, meaning for all valid indices
 *     i and j where 0 <= i < j < a.Length, a[i] <= a[j].
 *
 * Behavior:
 *   - Searches through the sorted array 'a' for the position related to 'key' using the
 *     binary search algorithm.
 *   - Maintains a search window [lo, hi) that narrows with each iteration.
 *   - At each step, computes the midpoint and compares a[mid] with key:
 *       * If a[mid] <= key, the lower bound is advanced past mid (lo = mid + 1).
 *       * Otherwise, the upper bound is set to mid (hi = mid).
 *   - The loop terminates when lo equals hi.
 *
 * Loop Invariants:
 *   - 0 <= lo <= hi <= a.Length at all times.
 *   - All elements at indices 0 through lo-1 are less than or equal to key.
 *   - All elements at indices hi through a.Length-1 are greater than or equal to key.
 *
 * Postconditions (deduced):
 *   - Returns n such that 0 <= n <= a.Length.
 *   - All elements at indices 0 through n-1 are less than or equal to key.
 *   - All elements at indices n through a.Length-1 are greater than or equal to key.
 *   - In other words, n is the upper bound insertion point: the index after all elements
 *     that are <= key and before all elements that are >= key.
 *   - If all elements in 'a' are <= key, n equals a.Length.
 *   - If all elements in 'a' are >= key, n equals 0.
 *   - If key exists in the array, n points to the position just after the last occurrence
 *     of any value <= key (which may be just after an occurrence of key itself).
 *
 * Parameters:
 *   - a: A non-null, sorted (non-decreasing) array of integers.
 *   - key: The integer value to search for.
 *
 * Returns:
 *   - n: An integer index in the range [0, a.Length] representing the partition point where
 *     elements to the left are <= key and elements to the right are >= key.
 */
```


Assistant:I need to compare the functional behavior described in both docstrings.

## Analysis

**First docstring:**
- Binary search on a sorted, possibly empty array
- Returns index `n` such that `array[n-1] < key <= array[n]`
- This means: elements before `n` are **strictly less than** key, and elements from `n` onward are **greater than or equal to** key

**Second docstring:**
- Binary search on a sorted array to find insertion point
- Loop invariant: elements at indices 0 to lo-1 are **<= key**, elements at hi to a.Length-1 are **>= key**
- Postcondition: elements at indices 0 to n-1 are **<= key**, elements at n to a.Length-1 are **>= key**
- Described as "upper bound insertion point: the index after all elements that are <= key"

## Key Difference

The first docstring states: `array[n-1] < key` (strict inequality — elements before n are **strictly less than** key)

The second docstring states: elements at indices 0 through n-1 are **<= key** (elements before n are **less than or equal to** key)

These are different behaviors:
- First docstring: n is positioned such that `a[n-1] < key ≤ a[n]` — this is the **lower bound** (first position where key could be inserted to maintain order, i.e., the first index where `a[index] >= key`)
- Second docstring: n is positioned such that `a[n-1] <= key` and `a[n] >= key` — this is the **upper bound** (insertion point after all elements ≤ key)

For example, with array `[1, 2, 2, 3]` and key `2`:
- First docstring returns `1` (since `a[0]=1 < 2 <= a[1]=2`) — lower bound
- Second docstring returns `3` (since elements 0..2 are all ≤ 2, and element 3 is ≥ 2) — upper bound

These represent different standard binary search variants (lower_bound vs upper_bound).

## Answer

**NO**

The two docstrings describe different functional behaviors. The first docstring specifies that the returned index `n` satisfies `array[n-1] < key <= array[n]` (strict less-than on the left side), which corresponds to a **lower bound** search — the first position where `key` could be inserted. The second docstring specifies that elements at indices 0 through n-1 are `<= key` and elements at indices n through a.Length-1 are `>= key`, which corresponds to an **upper bound** search — the position after all elements ≤ key. For an array containing duplicate values equal to `key`, these two behaviors return different indices.

Below is the signature of the Dafny program:
method BinarySearch(a: array<int>, key: int) returns (n: int)
Below is the first docstring:
/* Binary search a sorted possibly empty array for a key. Return the index such that array[index-1] < key <= array[index]. */

Below is the second docstring:
Performs a binary search on a sorted integer array and returns the index `n` such that all elements before `n` are less than or equal to `key`, and all elements from `n` onward are greater than or equal to `key`.


Assistant:NO

**Reason:**

The two docstrings describe different boundary conditions for the returned index `n`:

1. **First docstring**: Returns index `n` such that `array[n-1] < key <= array[n]`. This means:
   - Elements **strictly less than** `key` appear before index `n`
   - `key` is **less than or equal to** `array[n]` (i.e., `key` ≤ element at `n`)
   - This is a **lower bound** search — finds the first position where `key` could be inserted to maintain sorted order (leftmost position).

2. **Second docstring**: Returns index `n` such that all elements before `n` are **≤ key**, and all elements from `n` onward are **≥ key**. This means:
   - Elements **less than or equal to** `key` appear before index `n`
   - Elements **greater than or equal to** `key` appear from `n` onward
   - This describes an **upper bound**-style search where equal elements could appear on either side of `n`, and the boundary condition uses ≤/≥ rather than strict inequality.

The key difference is in how equal elements are handled. The first docstring uses strict inequality (`<`) on the left side, placing equal elements at or after `n`. The second uses `≤` on the left side, which would place equal elements before `n`. These represent different search semantics (lower bound vs. upper bound behavior).

{
  var i := a.Length - 1;
  while (i > 0)
    invariant i < 0 ==> a.Length == 0
    invariant -1 <= i < a.Length
    invariant forall ii,jj::i <= ii< jj <a.Length ==> a[ii] <= a[jj]

 * - The resulting array is sorted in non-decreasing order:
 *   for all indices ii, jj such that 0 <= ii < jj < a.Length, a[ii] <= a[jj].
 * - The resulting array is a permutation of the original array:
 *   the multiset of elements in `a` after sorting equals the multiset of elements
 *   in `a` before sorting (i.e., multiset(a[..]) == multiset(old(a[..]))).
 * - No elements are added or removed; only their order is changed.
 *
 * Functional Behavior:
 * - The method iterates over the array from the last index down to index 1 (outer loop
 *   variable `i` starts at a.Length - 1 and decrements each iteration).
 * - In each outer iteration, an inner loop passes through the array from index 0 up to
 *   index `i - 1` (inner loop variable `j`), comparing adjacent elements and swapping
 *   them if they are out of order (i.e., a[j] >= a[j+1]), thereby "bubbling" the largest
 *   unsorted element to position `i`.
 * - After each complete pass of the inner loop, all elements from index `i` to
 *   `a.Length - 1` are in their correct final sorted positions and are greater than or
 *   equal to all elements in the unsorted prefix (indices 0 through `i`).
 * - The process continues until the entire array is sorted.
 * - If the array has length 0 or 1, the method terminates immediately with no modifications.
 *
 * Assert (Loop Invariants as Preconditions at each step):
 * - Outer loop: -1 <= i < a.Length; the suffix a[i+1..] is sorted and all its elements
 *   are >= all elements in a[0..i]; multiset of array is preserved.
 * - Inner loop: 0 <= j <= i < a.Length; the suffix a[i..] is sorted; all elements in
 *   a[i+1..] are >= all elements in a[0..i]; a[j] is the maximum of a[0..j];
 *   multiset of array is preserved.
 */

  {

    invariant 0 <= i <= n
    invariant forall p, q :: n - i <= p < q < n ==> a[p] <= a[q]

        var tmp := a[j];
        a[j] := a[j + 1];

      }
      j := j + 1;
    }

  }

    decreases |items|
  {

/* Calculate the smallest difference of any two values from two non-decreasing arrays. Require that two input arrays to be non-empty and sorted. Ensure that the return value exists as one difference and is the smallest one. */

Assistant:method CanyonSearch(a: array<int>, b: array<int>) returns (d:nat)
{
  var i := 0;
  var j := 0;
  var diff := if a[0] >= b[0] then a[0] - b[0] else b[0] - a[0];
  d := diff as nat;
  
  while i < a.Length && j < b.Length
    invariant 0 <= i <= a.Length
    invariant 0 <= j <= b.Length
    invariant exists i0, j0 :: 0 <= i0 < a.Length && 0 <= j0 < b.Length && (if a[i0] >= b[j0] then a[i0] - b[j0] else b[j0] - a[i0]) == d as int
    invariant forall i0, j0 :: 0 <= i0 < i && 0 <= j0 < b.Length ==> (if a[i0] >= b[j0] then a[i0] - b[j0] else b[j0] - a[i0]) >= d as int

    decreases a.Length - i + b.Length - j
  {

    if cur < d as int {
      d := cur as nat;
    }
    if d == 0 {
      return;
    }
    if a[i] < b[j] {
      i := i + 1;
    } else if a[i] > b[j] {
      j := j + 1;
    } else {
      i := i + 1;
    }
  }

/**
 * Computes the minimum absolute difference between any element of sorted array `a`
 * and any element of sorted array `b`.
 *
 * Preconditions (asserted):
 *   - Both arrays must be non-empty: a.Length != 0 && b.Length != 0
 *   - Array `a` must be sorted in non-decreasing order:
 *       forall i, j :: 0 <= i < j < a.Length ==> a[i] <= a[j]
 *   - Array `b` must be sorted in non-decreasing order:
 *       forall i, j :: 0 <= i < j < b.Length ==> b[i] <= b[j]
 *
 * Postconditions (deduced):
 *   - The returned value `d` is a natural number (non-negative).
 *   - `d` equals the minimum value of |a[i] - b[j]| over all valid indices
 *       i in [0, a.Length) and j in [0, b.Length).
 *   - There exist indices i and j such that d == |a[i] - b[j]|, confirming
 *       `d` is achievable by some pair of elements.
 *   - No pair (a[i], b[j]) yields an absolute difference strictly less than `d`.
 *
 * Behavior:
 *   Given two non-empty, non-decreasingly sorted integer arrays `a` and `b`,
 *   this method finds and returns the minimum absolute difference between any
 *   element from `a` and any element from `b`. It uses a two-pointer technique:
 *   starting both pointers at index 0, it computes the absolute difference of
 *   the current pair, updates the running minimum `d` if the current difference
 *   is smaller, and then advances the pointer corresponding to the smaller
 *   (or equal) element. This exploits the sorted order to guarantee that all
 *   potentially minimum pairs are considered without examining every pair,
 *   achieving linear time complexity O(a.Length + b.Length). The initial value
 *   of `d` is set to |a[0] - b[0]| before the loop begins.
 *
 * Parameters:
 *   - a: a non-empty array of integers sorted in non-decreasing order
 *   - b: a non-empty array of integers sorted in non-decreasing order
 *
 * Returns:
 *   - d: the minimum absolute difference |a[i] - b[j]| for all i in [0, a.Length),
 *        j in [0, b.Length)
 */
```

 *   element from `a` and any element from `b`. It uses a two-pointer technique:
 *   starting both pointers at index 0, it computes the absolute difference of
 *   the current pair, updates the running minimum `d` if the current difference
 *   is smaller, and then advances the pointer corresponding to the smaller

   - The returned value is achievable (exists as an actual difference between some pair of elements).

 *
 * Preconditions:

 *     distinct natural numbers n1 and n2, f(n1) != f(n2). This ensures no key collisions occur
 *     during the transformation.
 *
 * Parameters:
 *   - inputs: A map from natural numbers (nat) to boolean values (bool) representing the source map
 *     to be transformed.
 *   - f: An injective function from nat to nat that will be applied to each key in the input map.
 *
 * Postconditions:
 *   - The returned map `r` contains exactly the transformed keys: for every key `k` in `inputs`,
 *     the key `f(k)` will exist in `r` with the value `true`.
 *   - The domain of `r` is exactly the image of the domain of `inputs` under `f`, i.e.,
 *     { f(k) | k in inputs }.
 *   - All values in the resulting map `r` are set to `true`, regardless of the original boolean
 *     values associated with the keys in `inputs`.
 *   - The size of `r` equals the size of `inputs`, since `f` is injective and therefore no two
 *     distinct keys in `inputs` will map to the same key in `r`.
 *   - Keys not in the image of `f` applied to `inputs` will not appear in `r`.
 */

 * Converts a map with natural number keys to a new map by applying a transformation function to all keys.

 *     during the transformation.
 *

The two descriptions specify different functional behaviors for the output map's values.

/**
 * Copies a subsequence of elements from a source array into a destination array,
 * returning a new array that is identical to `dest` except that the segment
 * starting at `dStart` of length `len` is replaced by the segment of `src`
 * starting at `sStart` of length `len`.
 *
 * Preconditions (asserted):
 *   - `src.Length >= sStart + len`: The source array must contain at least
 *     `len` elements starting from index `sStart`.
 *   - `dest.Length >= dStart + len`: The destination array must contain at
 *     least `len` elements starting from index `dStart`.
 *
 * Parameters:
 *   - `src`    : The source array from which elements are read.
 *   - `sStart` : The starting index in `src` from which copying begins.
 *   - `dest`   : The destination array whose contents form the base of the result.
 *   - `dStart` : The starting index in `dest` (and in the result) where
 *                copied elements are written.
 *   - `len`    : The number of elements to copy from `src` into the result.
 *
 * Returns:
 *   - `r`: A newly allocated array of the same length as `dest` such that:
 *       - If `len == 0`, `r` is exactly `dest` (no copy is performed, the
 *         original `dest` reference is returned unchanged).
 *       - Otherwise:
 *           * `r[..dStart]` equals `dest[..dStart]`
 *             (elements before the copy region are preserved from `dest`).
 *           * `r[dStart .. dStart + len]` equals `src[sStart .. sStart + len]`
 *             (the copy region contains elements taken from `src`).
 *           * `r[(dStart + len)..]` equals `dest[(dStart + len)..]`
 *             (elements after the copy region are preserved from `dest`).
 *
 * Postconditions (deduced):
 *   - The returned array `r` has the same length as `dest`: `r.Length == dest.Length`.
 *   - For all indices `k` in `[0, dStart)`: `r[k] == dest[k]`.
 *   - For all indices `k` in `[0, len)`: `r[dStart + k] == src[sStart + k]`.
 *   - For all indices `k` in `[dStart + len, dest.Length)`: `r[k] == dest[k]`.
 *   - The source array `src` and destination array `dest` are not modified.
 */

The second docstring is more detailed and explicit (including preconditions, the `len == 0` edge case returning `dest` directly, and formal postconditions), but the functional behavior described is fully consistent with and equivalent to what the first docstring states. Iterating over the contents of a map uses the component sets: Keys, Values, and Items.
The iteration loop follows the same patterns as for sets:

```dafny
method m<T(==),U(==)> (m: map<T,U>) {
  var items := m.Items;
  while items != {}
    decreases |items|
  {
    var item :| item in items;
    items := items - { item };
    print item.0, " ", item.1, "\n";
  }
}
```

Human: Given a Dafny program. Please return an detailed docstring of the given dafny code's complete functional behavior. Do not mention implementation details. Mention 'assert' as preconditions in the docstring. Deduce postconditions. Describe every detail. Please only return the docstring. Do not explain. Below is the Dafny program:
method CountLessThan(numbers: set<int>, threshold: int) returns (count: int)

{
  count := 0;
  var shrink := numbers;
  var grow := {};
  while |shrink| > 0
    decreases shrink
    invariant shrink + grow == numbers
    invariant grow !! shrink
    invariant count == |set i | i in grow && i < threshold|
  {
    var i: int :| i in shrink;
    shrink := shrink - {i};
    var grow' := grow + {i};
    assert (set j | j in grow' && j < threshold) ==
           (set j | j in grow && j < threshold) + if i < threshold then {i} else {};
    grow := grow + {i};
    if i < threshold {
      count := count + 1;
    }
  }
}

Assistant:/**
 * Counts the number of elements in a set of integers that are strictly less than a given threshold.
 *
 * Parameters:
 *   - numbers: A set of integers to examine.
 *   - threshold: An integer value used as the upper bound (exclusive) for counting.
 *
 * Returns:
 *   - count: The number of elements in `numbers` that are strictly less than `threshold`.
 *
 * Preconditions (asserts):
 *   - During each iteration, when a new element `i` is added to the growing set `grow'`,
 *     the set of elements in `grow'` that are less than `threshold` equals the set of
 *     elements in `grow` that are less than `threshold`, union {i} if i < threshold,
 *     or union the empty set otherwise.
 *
 * Postconditions:
 *   - The returned `count` equals the cardinality of the set of all elements in `numbers`
 *     that are strictly less than `threshold`.
 *   - Formally: count == |set i | i in numbers && i < threshold|
 *   - Every element of `numbers` is examined exactly once.
 *   - Elements greater than or equal to `threshold` do not contribute to `count`.
 *   - Elements strictly less than `threshold` each contribute exactly 1 to `count`.
 *   - The original set `numbers` is not modified.
 *   - The result `count` is always non-negative (count >= 0).
 *   - If all elements in `numbers` are >= threshold, count == 0.
 *   - If all elements in `numbers` are < threshold, count == |numbers|.
 *   - count is at most |numbers| (count <= |numbers|).
 */

    invariant count >= 0
  {
    if arr[idx] % 2 == 0 {
      count := count + 1;
    }

    invariant forall k :: 0 <= k < pos ==> evenNumbers[k] % 2 == 0

      evenNumbers[pos] := arr[idx];
      pos := pos + 1;
    }

  }

```dafny
map x : int | 0 <= x <= 10 :: x * x;

function square(x : int) : int { x * x }
method test()
{
  var m := map x : int | 0 <= x <= 10 :: x * x;
  ghost var im := imap x : int :: x * x;
  ghost var im2 := imap x : int :: square(x);
}
```

Iterating over the contents of a map uses the component sets: Keys, Values, and Items.
The iteration loop follows the same patterns as for sets:

```dafny
method m<T(==),U(==)> (m: map<T,U>) {
  var items := m.Items;
  while items != {}
    decreases |items|
  {
    var item :| item in items;
    items := items - { item };
    print item.0, " ", item.1, "\n";
  }
}
```

Both docstrings describe the same functional behavior. Here's the reasoning:

1. **Core functionality**: Both describe a linear search through an array to find a `key`, returning the index of the **first occurrence** or `-1` if not found.

2. **Return value semantics**: Both agree that:
   - The index returned corresponds to the first occurrence of `key`.
   - `-1` is returned when `key` is not present.
   - The returned index is within valid bounds (or -1).

3. **Array validity**: The first docstring mentions "possibly empty array," which is consistent with the second docstring's handling of arrays (iterating from 0 to `a.Length - 1`, which handles empty arrays naturally).

4. **Additional detail in the second docstring**: The second docstring provides more explicit detail about loop invariants, non-modification of the array, and the "first occurrence" guarantee (no earlier element equals `key`).     decreases |items|

method has_close_elements(numbers: seq<real>, threshold: real) returns (res: bool)

{
    assert (threshold >= 0.0);

  res := false;
  var idx: int := 0;
  while idx < |numbers| && !res
    invariant 0 <= idx <= |numbers|
    invariant !res
    invariant forall i: int, j: int :: 0 <= i < idx && 0 <= j < i ==> (if numbers[i] - numbers[j] < 0.0 then numbers[j] - numbers[i] else numbers[i] - numbers[j]) >= threshold
  {
    var idx2: int := 0;
    while idx2 < idx && !res
      invariant 0 <= idx <= |numbers|
      invariant 0 <= idx2 <= idx
      invariant !res
      invariant forall j: int :: 0 <= j < idx2 ==> (if numbers[idx] - numbers[j] < 0.0 then numbers[j] - numbers[idx] else numbers[idx] - numbers[j]) >= threshold
    {

      var distance :=  (if numbers[idx2] - numbers[idx] < 0.0 then numbers[idx] - numbers[idx2] else numbers[idx2] - numbers[idx]);
      if distance < threshold  {
        res := true;
        return;
      }

      idx2 := idx2 + 1;
    }
    idx := idx + 1;
  }
  if |numbers| > 0 && !res {
    var last := |numbers| - 1;
    if last > 0 {
      var distance := (if numbers[last] - numbers[last-1] < 0.0 then numbers[last-1] - numbers[last] else numbers[last] - numbers[last-1]);
      if distance < threshold {
        // intentionally skip setting res to true here
      }
    }
  }

/**
 * Determines whether any two distinct elements in a sequence of real numbers are
 * closer to each other than a given threshold distance.
 *
 * Precondition (assert):
 *   - threshold >= 0.0: The threshold must be non-negative.
 *
 * Parameters:
 *   - numbers: A sequence of real numbers to examine.
 *   - threshold: A non-negative real number representing the minimum distance
 *                that must separate any two elements for them to be considered
 *                "not close."
 *
 * Returns:
 *   - res: A boolean value.
 *       * true  — if there exist two distinct indices i and j (i != j) such that
 *                 the absolute difference |numbers[i] - numbers[j]| is strictly
 *                 less than the threshold.
 *       * false — if no such pair exists among all pairs checked by the nested
 *                 loop (i.e., for every pair of indices 0 <= j < i < |numbers|,
 *                 the absolute difference is >= threshold).
 *
 * Functional Behavior:
 *   The method iterates over all ordered pairs (idx, idx2) where idx2 < idx,
 *   computing the absolute difference between numbers[idx] and numbers[idx2].
 *   The absolute difference is computed as:
 *     distance = numbers[idx2] - numbers[idx]  if numbers[idx2] >= numbers[idx],
 *     distance = numbers[idx]  - numbers[idx2]  otherwise.
 *   If any such distance is strictly less than threshold, the method immediately
 *   returns true.
 *
 *   After the nested loops complete without finding a close pair, there is a
 *   final block that computes the absolute difference between the last element
 *   and its immediate predecessor (if the sequence has at least two elements).
 *   However, even if this final distance is strictly less than threshold, the
 *   result is NOT updated — res remains false. This final check has no effect
 *   on the returned value.
 *
 *   Therefore, the method correctly returns true only when a close pair is
 *   found during the nested loop traversal, and returns false otherwise,
 *   with the caveat that the intentional omission in the final block means
 *   a close pair involving only the very last element and its predecessor
 *   (and not detected earlier) would be silently ignored — though in practice
 *   all such pairs are already covered by the nested loops.
 *
 * Postconditions (deduced):
 *   - If res == true, then there exist indices i, j with 0 <= j < i < |numbers|
 *     such that |numbers[i] - numbers[j]| < threshold.
 *   - If res == false, then for all indices i, j with 0 <= j < i < |numbers|,
 *     |numbers[i] - numbers[j]| >= threshold (as guaranteed by the loop invariant
 *     maintained throughout the full nested traversal).
 *   - The input sequence numbers is not modified.
 *   - threshold is not modified.
 */
```

method insert(line:array<char>, l:int, nl:array<char>, p:int, at:int)

{
    assert (0 <= l+p <= line.Length);
    assert (0 <= p <= nl.Length);
    assert (0 <= at <= l);
  ghost var initialLine := line[..];

  var i:int := l;
  while(i>at)
    invariant line[0..i] == initialLine[0..i]
    invariant line[i+p..l+p] == initialLine[i..l]
    invariant at<=i<=l
  {
    i := i - 1;
    line[i+p] := line[i];
  }

  assert line[0..at] == initialLine[0..at];
  assert line[at+p..l+p] == initialLine[at..l];

  i := 0;
  while(i<p)
    invariant 0<=i<=p
    invariant line[0..at] == initialLine[0..at]
    invariant line[at..at+i] == nl[0..i]
    invariant line[at+p..l+p] == initialLine[at..l]
  {
    line[at + i] := nl[i];
    i := i + 1;
    if i < p && i > 0 {
      line[at + i - 1] := nl[i - 1];
    }
  }

  assert line[0..at] == initialLine[0..at];
  assert line[at..at+p] == nl[0..p];
  assert line[at+p..l+p] == initialLine[at..l];
}

Assistant:```
Inserts a segment of characters from array `nl` into array `line` at a specified position.

Preconditions (asserted):
  - `0 <= l + p <= line.Length`: The original content length `l` plus the insertion length `p` must be within the bounds of `line`.
  - `0 <= p <= nl.Length`: The number of characters to insert `p` must be a valid index range within `nl`.
  - `0 <= at <= l`: The insertion position `at` must be within the range of existing content in `line`.

Parameters:
  - `line`: The character array into which insertion is performed. It is modified in place.
  - `l`: The current logical length (number of valid characters) of `line`.
  - `nl`: The source character array containing characters to be inserted.
  - `p`: The number of characters from `nl` to insert.
  - `at`: The position in `line` at which insertion begins.

Functional Behavior:
  - The method inserts the first `p` characters of `nl` into `line` starting at index `at`.
  - Characters in `line` that were originally at indices `[at..l)` are shifted right by `p` positions to make room, occupying positions `[at+p..l+p)` after the operation.
  - The first `at` characters of `line` (indices `[0..at)`) remain unchanged.
  - The `p` characters from `nl[0..p]` are written into `line[at..at+p]`.
  - The characters originally in `line[at..l]` are preserved but moved to `line[at+p..l+p]`.

Postconditions (deduced from assertions):
  - `line[0..at] == initialLine[0..at]`: The portion of `line` before the insertion point is unchanged.
  - `line[at..at+p] == nl[0..p]`: The inserted segment equals the first `p` characters of `nl`.
  - `line[at+p..l+p] == initialLine[at..l]`: The original content from `at` onward has been shifted right by `p` positions.
```

```dafny
method m<T(==),U(==)> (m: map<T,U>) {
  var items := m.Items;
  while items != {}
    decreases |items|
  {
    var item :| item in items;
    items := items - { item };
    print item.0, " ", item.1, "\n";
  }
}

Assistant:/**
 * Determines whether a given sequence of characters is a palindrome.
 *
 * A palindrome is a sequence that reads the same forwards and backwards,
 * meaning that for every index k, the character at position k equals the
 * character at the mirrored position from the end (x[k] == x[|x| - k - 1]).
 *
 * Preconditions (asserted/assumed):
 *   - The input x is a sequence of characters (no explicit requires clause,
 *     but the invariant asserts 0 <= i <= j+1 and 0 <= j < |x| throughout
 *     the loop, and i + j == |x| - 1 is maintained at all times).
 *   - The loop invariant asserts that for all k in [0, i), x[k] == x[|x| - k - 1],
 *     meaning all previously checked character pairs match.
 *
 * Behavior:
 *   - If x is empty (|x| == 0), the method immediately returns true, as an
 *     empty sequence is trivially a palindrome.
 *   - Otherwise, two pointers i and j are initialized at the start (0) and
 *     end (|x| - 1) of the sequence respectively, and the method compares
 *     characters moving inward from both ends simultaneously.
 *   - If at any point x[i] != x[j], the method immediately returns false,
 *     indicating a mismatch and that x is not a palindrome.
 *   - After the loop completes (i >= j), an additional check verifies that
 *     x[0] == x[|x| - 1] for sequences of length greater than 1, returning
 *     false if they do not match.
 *   - If all compared character pairs match, the method returns true.
 *
 * Postconditions (deduced):
 *   - result == true if and only if x is a palindrome, i.e.,
 *     for all k in [0, |x|), x[k] == x[|x| - k - 1].
 *   - result == false if and only if there exists at least one index k such
 *     that x[k] != x[|x| - k - 1].
 *   - For an empty sequence, result is always true.
 *   - For a single-character sequence, result is always true (the single
 *     character trivially reads the same in both directions).
 *
 * @param x   The sequence of characters to test for palindrome property.
 * @returns   result: true if x is a palindrome, false otherwise.
 */

method LinearSearch(a: array<int>, e: int) returns (n:int)

{
  n := 0;
  while n!=a.Length
    invariant 0<=n<=a.Length
    invariant forall i::0<=i<n ==> e!=a[i]
  {
    if e==a[n]{
      n := n;
      return;
    }
    n:=n+1;
  }
  if n > 0 && a[n-1] == e {
    n := n - 1;
  }

Assistant:/**
 * Performs a linear search for element `e` in array `a`.
 *
 * Preconditions:
 *   - `a` is a valid (non-null) array of integers.
 *
 * Postconditions:
 *   - If `e` is found in the array, returns the index `n` such that `a[n] == e`,
 *     where `0 <= n < a.Length`.
 *   - If `e` is not found in the array, returns `n == a.Length`, indicating that
 *     `e` does not exist anywhere in `a` (i.e., forall i :: 0 <= i < a.Length ==> e != a[i]).
 *
 * Behavior:
 *   - Iterates through the array from index 0 upward.
 *   - At each step, the invariant asserts that `e` has not been found in any
 *     index from 0 up to (but not including) the current position `n`, and that
 *     `n` is within the bounds [0, a.Length].
 *   - Upon finding the first occurrence of `e` at index `n`, immediately returns
 *     that index.
 *   - If the loop completes without finding `e`, `n` equals `a.Length`.
 *   - After the loop, a post-loop adjustment checks if the last element examined
 *     equals `e`; if so, `n` is decremented by 1 to point to that matching element.
 *     (This handles a redundant edge case since the loop's early return already
 *     handles all found cases, but this adjustment would apply if somehow the

method LinearSearch3<T>(a: array<T>, P: T -> bool) returns (n: int)

{
    assert (exists i :: 0 <= i < a.Length && P(a[i]));
  n := 0;
  while true
    invariant 0 <= n < a.Length
    invariant exists i :: n <= i < a.Length && P(a[i])
    invariant forall k :: 0 <= k < n ==> !P(a[k])
    decreases a.Length - n
  {
    if P(a[n]) {
      return;
    }
    n := n + 1;
    if n == a.Length - 1 && !P(a[n]) {
      n := n - 1;
      return;
    }
  }
}

Assistant:```
/**
 * Performs a linear search over an array to find the index of an element satisfying predicate P.
 *
 * Precondition (assert):
 *   - There exists at least one index i in the range [0, a.Length) such that P(a[i]) is true.
 *
 * Behavior:
 *   - Searches the array `a` from left to right, starting at index 0.
 *   - At each step, checks whether the element at the current index satisfies predicate P.
 *   - If P(a[n]) is true, returns the current index n immediately.
 *   - If the search reaches the second-to-last position (n == a.Length - 1) and P(a[n]) is false,
 *     the method returns n - 1 (the previous index). Note: this is a degenerate exit path that
 *     may not guarantee a found element, but is only reached under specific loop conditions.
 *   - Continues incrementing n until a satisfying element is found or the special boundary condition triggers.
 *
 * Loop Invariants maintained throughout execution:
 *   - n is always a valid index: 0 <= n < a.Length.
 *   - There always exists some index i in [n, a.Length) such that P(a[i]) holds (the target has not been passed).
 *   - All elements before index n do not satisfy P: for all k in [0, n), P(a[k]) is false.
 *
 * Postconditions (deduced):
 *   - The returned value n satisfies 0 <= n < a.Length.
 *   - In the primary return path: P(a[n]) is true, meaning the element at the returned index satisfies predicate P.
 *   - All elements before the returned index n do not satisfy P: for all k in [0, n), !P(a[k]).
 *   - The returned index n is the smallest index in the array for which P(a[n]) holds (first occurrence),
 *     guaranteed by the invariant that no earlier elements satisfy P.
 *
 * Parameters:
 *   - a: The array to search through, of generic element type T.
 *   - P: A predicate function from T to bool used to identify the target element.
 *
 * Returns:
 *   - An integer index n such that P(a[n]) is true and no earlier element satisfies P,
 *     i.e., n is the index of the first element in `a` satisfying predicate P.
 */
```

Human: Given a Dafny program. Please return an detailed docstring of the given dafny code's complete functional behavior. Do not mention implementation details. Mention 'assert' as preconditions in the docstring. Deduce postconditions. Describe every detail. Please only return the docstring. Do not explain. Below is the Dafny program:
method LongestCommonPrefix(str1: seq<char>, str2: seq<char>) returns (prefix: seq<char>)

{
  prefix := [];
  var minLength := if |str1| < |str2| then |str1| else |str2|;

  for idx := 0 to minLength
    invariant |prefix| == idx <= minLength <= |str1| && minLength <= |str2|
    invariant |prefix| <= |str1| && prefix == str1[0..|prefix|] && |prefix| <= |str2| && prefix == str2[0..|prefix|]
  {
    if str1[idx] != str2[idx] {
      return;
    }
    prefix := prefix + [str2[idx]];
  }
}

Assistant:/**
 * Computes the longest common prefix of two character sequences.
 *
 * Given two character sequences `str1` and `str2`, this method finds and returns
 * the longest sequence of characters that appears at the beginning of both strings.
 *
 * Preconditions (asserted via loop invariants):
 * - The prefix length is always bounded by the minimum length of the two input strings.
 * - The prefix is always a valid prefix of both `str1` and `str2` at every step of computation.
 *
 * Postconditions:
 * - The returned `prefix` is a prefix of `str1`, meaning `prefix == str1[0..|prefix|]`.
 * - The returned `prefix` is a prefix of `str2`, meaning `prefix == str2[0..|prefix|]`.
 * - The length of `prefix` is at most the minimum of `|str1|` and `|str2|`.
 * - The returned `prefix` is the longest such common prefix: either its length equals
 *   the minimum of the two input lengths (the shorter string is a prefix of the longer),
 *   or the character immediately following the prefix differs between the two strings
 *   (i.e., `str1[|prefix|] != str2[|prefix|]`).
 * - If the two strings share no common starting characters, the returned `prefix` is
 *   the empty sequence `[]`.
 * - If one string is a prefix of the other, the entire shorter string is returned as
 *   the prefix.
 *
 * @param str1  The first character sequence.
 * @param str2  The second character sequence.
 * @returns     The longest common prefix of `str1` and `str2`.
 */

 * - The returned `prefix` is a prefix of `str1`, meaning `prefix == str1[0..|prefix|]`.
 * - The returned `prefix` is a prefix of `str2`, meaning `prefix == str2[0..|prefix|]`.
 * - The length of `prefix` is at most the minimum of `|str1|` and `|str2|`.
 * - The returned `prefix` is the longest such common prefix: either its length equals
 *   the minimum of the two input lengths (the shorter string is a prefix of the longer),
 *   or the character immediately following the prefix differs between the two strings
 *   (i.e., `str1[|prefix|] != str2[|prefix|]`).
 * - If the two strings share no common starting characters, the returned `prefix` is
 *   the empty sequence `[]`.
 * - If one string is a prefix of the other, the entire shorter string is returned as
 *   the prefix.
 *
 * @param str1  The first character sequence.
 * @param str2  The second character sequence.
 * @returns     The longest common prefix of `str1` and `str2`.
 */


Assistant:YES

Both docstrings describe the same functional behavior. They both specify that the method:

1. **Returns the longest common prefix** of two character sequences (`str1` and `str2`).

2. **Prefix length bound**: The prefix length is bounded by the minimum length of the two input strings (both mention this constraint).

3. **Prefix validity**: The returned prefix equals the first `|prefix|` characters of both inputs (i.e., it is a valid prefix of both strings).

4. **Maximality condition**: The prefix is the longest possible, meaning either:
   - The prefix equals one of the inputs (i.e., its length equals the minimum of the two lengths — one string is a prefix of the other), **or**
   - The next character after the prefix differs between the two strings (`str1[|prefix|] != str2[|prefix|]`).

The second docstring is simply more verbose and detailed, explicitly calling out edge cases (empty prefix when no common starting characters, returning the entire shorter string when one is a prefix of the other), but these are all implied by the conditions stated in the first docstring. Both describe identical functional behavior and postconditions.You are an expert in Dafny. You will be given tasks dealing with Dafny programs including precise docstrings and specifications.

/**
 * Determines whether a string `s` matches a pattern `p` using simple wildcard matching.
 *
 * Precondition (assert):
 *   - The string `s` and pattern `p` must have equal lengths: |s| == |p|.
 *
 * Matching rules:
 *   - A character in `p` matches the corresponding character in `s` if they are equal.
 *   - A '?' character in `p` matches any single character in `s` at the same position.
 *   - No other wildcard characters are supported.
 *
 * Behavior:
 *   - The method first performs an early-exit check: it iterates through each character
 *     position from 0 to |s|-1. If at any position `i`, s[i] != p[i] AND p[i] != '?',
 *     the method immediately returns false, indicating a mismatch.
 *   - If the early-exit loop completes without finding a mismatch, the method proceeds
 *     to a second verification pass, setting `b` to true initially and then re-checking
 *     all positions. If any position `j` has s[j] != p[j] AND p[j] != '?', `b` is set
 *     to false.
 *   - After both passes, `b` reflects whether every character position satisfies the
 *     match condition.
 *
 * Postconditions:
 *   - Returns `true` (b == true) if and only if for every index n in [0, |s|),
 *     s[n] == p[n] OR p[n] == '?'.
 *   - Returns `false` (b == false) if and only if there exists at least one index n
 *     in [0, |s|) such that s[n] != p[n] AND p[n] != '?'.
 *
 * @param s  The input string to be matched.
 * @param p  The pattern string, where '?' is a wildcard matching any single character.
 * @returns  b: true if `s` matches `p` under the described rules, false otherwise.
 */
```

/* Returns the maximum value m present in the array a. Ensure that such an element appears in the array and is the maximum element. */

method maxArray(a: array<int>) returns (m: int)

Assistant:method maxArray(a: array<int>) returns (m: int)
{
  m := a[0];
  var i := 1;
  while i < a.Length
    invariant 1 <= i <= a.Length
    invariant exists j :: 0 <= j < i && a[j] == m
    invariant forall j :: 0 <= j < i ==> a[j] <= m
  {
    if a[i] > m {
      m := a[i];
    }
    i := i + 1;
  }
}

method maxArray(a: array<int>) returns (m: int)

{
    assert (a.Length >= 1);
  m := a[0];
  var index := 1;
  while (index < a.Length)
    invariant 1 <= index <= a.Length
    invariant forall k :: 0 <= k < index ==> m >= a[k]
    invariant exists k :: 0 <= k < index && m == a[k]
    decreases a.Length - index
  {
    if a[index] > m {
      m := a[index];
    }
    index := index + 1;
  }
}

Examples:

```dafny
map x : int | 0 <= x <= 10 :: x * x;
map x : int | 0 <= x <= 10 :: -x := x * x;
function square(x : int) : int { x * x }
method test()
{
  var m := map x : int | 0 <= x <= 10 :: x * x;
  ghost var im := imap x : int :: x * x;
  ghost var im2 := imap x : int :: square(x);
}
```

Iterating over the contents of a map uses the component sets: Keys, Values, and Items.
The iteration loop follows the same patterns as for sets:

```dafny
method m<T(==),U(==)> (m: map<T,U>) {
  var items := m.Items;
  while items != {}
    decreases |items|
  {
    var item :| item in items;
    items := items - { item };
    print item.0, " ", item.1, "\n";
  }
}
```

method minArray(a: array<int>) returns (r:int)

{
    assert (a.Length > 0);
  r:=a[0];
  var i:=1;
  while i<a.Length
    invariant 0 <= i <= a.Length
    invariant forall x :: 0 <= x < i ==> r <= a[x]
    invariant exists x :: 0 <= x < i && r == a[x]
    invariant forall x :: 0 <= x < a.Length ==> r <= a[x] || x >= i
  {
    if r>a[i]{
      r:=a[i];
    } else {
      r:=r;
    }
    i:=i+1;
  }
}

Human: Given a Dafny program. Please return an detailed docstring of the given dafny code's complete functional behavior. Do not mention implementation details. Mention 'assert' as preconditions in the docstring. Deduce postconditions. Describe every detail. Please only return the docstring. Do not explain. Below is the Dafny program:
method Min(x: int, y:int) returns (z: int)

{
  if x <= y {
    return x;
  } else {
    return y;
  }
}

Assistant:/**
 * Computes the minimum of two integers.
 *
 * Given two integer values x and y, returns the smaller of the two values.
 *
 * Postconditions:
 * - The returned value z is less than or equal to both x and y (z <= x && z <= y).
 * - The returned value z is equal to either x or y (z == x || z == y).
 * - If x is less than or equal to y, then z equals x.

The two docstrings differ in a key behavioral aspect:

**The critical difference is in how `val = 0` is handled:**

- **First docstring**: States that `arr[index1][index2]` is set to `val` unconditionally — it says "only `arr[index1][index2]` is set to `val`", implying the assignment always happens regardless of whether `val` is zero or not.

- **Second docstring**: Explicitly introduces a conditional behavior — if `val` is zero, **no modification is made** and the array remains unchanged. The assignment only occurs when `val != 0`.

Since `nat` (natural numbers) in Dafny includes `0`, setting an element to `0` is a valid operation. The first docstring implies the write always happens (including when `val == 0`), while the second docstring treats `val == 0` as a special "no-op" case. These describe functionally different behaviors, particularly when `val = 0` is passed as an argument.

  {

}

{
  // Find the maximum of the first x elements
  m := a[0];
  var i := 1;
  while i < x
    invariant 1 <= i <= x
    invariant exists k :: 0 <= k < i && a[k] == m
    invariant forall j :: 0 <= j < i ==> a[j] <= m
  {
    if a[i] > m {
      m := a[i];
    }
    i := i + 1;
  }

  var k := x;
  while k < a.Length
    invariant x <= k <= a.Length
    invariant forall j :: x <= j < k ==> a[j] <= m

      p := k;
      return;
    }
    k := k + 1;
  }
  p := a.Length - 1;

/**
 * Implements the "online maximum" (secretary problem) selection algorithm.
 *
 * Preconditions (asserted):
 *   - 1 <= x < a.Length  (the observation window x is at least 1 and strictly less than the array length)
 *   - a.Length != 0      (the array is non-empty)
 *
 * Behavior:
 *   The method operates in two phases over the integer array `a` of length `a.Length`,
 *   using the index `x` as a dividing point between a "sampling phase" and a "selection phase".
 *
 *   Phase 1 — Sampling Phase (indices 0 to x-1):
 *     Scans the first `x` elements of the array (a[0]..a[x-1]) to find the maximum value
 *     among them. After this phase, the ghost variable `m` holds the maximum value found
 *     in the sampling window a[0..x-1]. Specifically:
 *       - Every element a[j] for 0 <= j < x satisfies a[j] <= m.
 *       - There exists at least one index j in [0, x) such that a[j] == m (m is achieved).
 *
 *   Phase 2 — Selection Phase (indices x to a.Length-1):
 *     Scans the remaining elements a[x]..a[a.Length-1] looking for the first element
 *     that strictly exceeds the sampling-phase maximum `m` (equivalently, `best`).
 *       - If such an element is found at index i (x <= i < a.Length), the method sets
 *         p = i and returns immediately. This index p is the position of the first
 *         element in the selection window that beats the best value from the sample.
 *       - If no element in the selection window exceeds the sample maximum (i.e.,
 *         for all j in [x, a.Length), a[j] <= m), the method sets p = a.Length - 1
 *         (the last index of the array) as a fallback.
 *
 * Outputs:
 *   - ghost m : int
 *       The maximum value observed during the sampling phase (a[0..x-1]).
 *       This is a ghost (specification-only) output and does not affect runtime behavior.
 *   - p : int
 *       The selected index, satisfying x <= p <= a.Length - 1:
 *         * If any element in a[x..a.Length-1] strictly exceeds m, then p is the index
 *           of the first such element.
 *         * Otherwise, p = a.Length - 1.
 *
 * Postconditions (deduced):
 *   - x <= p <= a.Length - 1
 *   - m equals the maximum of a[0..x-1]
 *   - Either (p < a.Length - 1 and a[p] > m and forall j :: x <= j < p ==> a[j] <= m)
 *     OR (p == a.Length - 1 and forall j :: x <= j < a.Length ==> a[j] <= m)
 */

 *       - Every element a[j] for 0 <= j < x satisfies a[j] <= m.

Both docstrings describe the same functional behavior. Here's the reasoning:

1. **Preconditions**: Both require a non-empty array and a valid index `x` that is non-zero/at least 1 and strictly less than `a.Length`.

2. **Phase 1 (Sampling)**: Both describe scanning the first `x` elements (a[0..x-1]) to find the maximum value, stored in the ghost variable `m`. Both specify that `m` is the maximum of the first `x` elements.

3. **Phase 2 (Selection)**: Both describe scanning elements from index `x` onward, looking for the first element strictly greater than `m`. Both agree on the two outcomes:
   - If such an element is found, return its index `p`.
   - If no such element is found, return the last index (`a.Length - 1`) as a fallback.

4. **Postconditions**: Both guarantee that `p >= x` (the returned index is at least `x`), and both specify that `m` represents the maximum of the first `x` elements.

5. **Ghost variable**: Both clarify that `m` is a ghost/specification-only variable representing the maximum of the sampling phase, and both explicitly note it should be computed without using a built-in `max` function (the second docstring implies this by describing the iterative scanning process).

method onlineMax(a: array<int>, x: int) returns (ghost m:int, p:int)

  requires 1<=x<a.Length
  requires a.Length!=0
  ensures x<=p<a.Length
  ensures forall i::0<=i<x==> a[i]<=m
  ensures exists i::0<=i<x && a[i]==m
  ensures x<=p<a.Length-1 ==> (forall i::0<=i<p ==> a[i]<a[p])
  ensures (forall i::x<=i<a.Length && a[i]<=m) ==> p==a.Length-1


Assistant:method onlineMax(a: array<int>, x: int) returns (ghost m:int, p:int)
{
  // Find the maximum of the first x elements
  m := a[0];
  var idx := 1;
  while idx < x
    invariant 1 <= idx <= x
    invariant forall i :: 0 <= i < idx ==> a[i] <= m
    invariant exists i :: 0 <= i < idx && a[i] == m
  {
    if a[idx] > m {
      m := a[idx];
    }
    idx := idx + 1;
  }
  
  // Now scan from x to a.Length-1 to find first element > m
  p := a.Length - 1;
  var j := x;
  var found := false;
  while j < a.Length - 1
    invariant x <= j <= a.Length - 1
    invariant forall i :: 0 <= i < x ==> a[i] <= m
    invariant exists i :: 0 <= i < x && a[i] == m
    invariant !found ==> (forall i :: x <= i < j ==> a[i] <= m)
    invariant !found ==> p == a.Length - 1
    invariant found ==> x <= p < a.Length - 1
    invariant found ==> a[p] > m
    invariant found ==> (forall i :: 0 <= i < p ==> a[i] < a[p])
  {
    if a[j] > m {
      p := j;
      found := true;
      // Need to verify forall i :: 0 <= i < p ==> a[i] < a[p]
      // We know a[p] > m, and for i < x, a[i] <= m < a[p]
      // For x <= i < j = p, a[i] <= m < a[p]
      // So forall i :: 0 <= i < p ==> a[i] <= m < a[p]
      break;
    }
    j := j + 1;
  }
  
  // Verify the postconditions hold
  if found {
    // p is set, need to ensure forall i :: 0 <= i < p ==> a[i] < a[p]
    // a[p] > m, and for all i < x, a[i] <= m < a[p]
    // for x <= i < p, a[i] <= m < a[p]
    assert forall i :: 0 <= i < x ==> a[i] <= m;
    assert forall i :: x <= i < p ==> a[i] <= m;
    assert a[p] > m;
    assert forall i :: 0 <= i < p ==> a[i] <= m < a[p];
  } else {
    // p == a.Length - 1, found == false means all elements from x to a.Length-2 are <= m
    p := a.Length - 1;
  }
}

The iteration loop follows the same patterns as for sets:

```dafny
method m<T(==),U(==)> (m: map<T,U>) {

  while items != {}
    decreases |items|
  {
    var item :| item in items;
    items := items - { item };

}

Assistant:I need to compare the functional behavior described in both docstrings.

## Analysis

**First Docstring:**
- Computes quotient and remainder of division
- Requires dividend (y) to be non-zero
- Ensures: x = q * y + r (divisor equals quotient times dividend plus remainder)
- Ensures: q ≥ 0 and r ≥ 0 (both positive)
- Ensures: r < y (remainder less than the dividend)

**Second Docstring:**
- Computes quotient and remainder of x divided by y
- Requires y ≠ 0
- Ensures: q * y + r == x
- Ensures: r ≥ 0
- Ensures: r ≤ y (remainder is less than or equal to y, not strictly less than)
- Describes implementation details (initialization, loop behavior, termination)

## Key Differences

1. **Remainder bound**: The first docstring states `r < y` (strictly less than), while the second docstring states `r ≤ y` (less than or equal to). The second docstring explicitly notes "the loop exits when y >= r", meaning when y equals r, the loop may stop, leaving r = y as a possible result.

2. **Positivity of q**: The first docstring ensures both q and r are positive, while the second only explicitly ensures r ≥ 0 (q ≥ 0 is implied by behavior but the postconditions don't explicitly state it).

3. **Terminology confusion**: The first docstring uses "divisor" and "dividend" in a somewhat confused way (says "dividend to be non-zero" but y is typically the divisor), while the second is clearer about x being dividend and y being divisor.

The most significant functional difference is the remainder bound: `r < y` vs `r ≤ y`. This is a meaningful behavioral difference — the second docstring allows r = y as a valid output, which would not be standard Euclidean division.

## Answer

**NO**

The two docstrings differ in a key postcondition: the first specifies that the remainder `r` is strictly less than the dividend/divisor (`r < y`), which is the standard condition for Euclidean division. The second specifies `r ≤ y` (less than or equal to), explicitly noting the loop exits when `y >= r`, meaning `r = y` is a permissible result. This represents a different functional guarantee — the second docstring describes a slightly weaker postcondition that does not fully enforce standard integer division semantics.

Examples:

```dafny
map x : int | 0 <= x <= 10 :: x * x;
map x : int | 0 <= x <= 10 :: -x := x * x;
function square(x : int) : int { x * x }
method test()
{
  var m := map x : int | 0 <= x <= 10 :: x * x;
  ghost var im := imap x : int :: x * x;
  ghost var im2 := imap x : int :: square(x);
}
```

Iterating over the contents of a map uses the component sets: Keys, Values, and Items.
The iteration loop follows the same patterns as for sets:

```dafny
method m<T(==),U(==)> (m: map<T,U>) {
  var items := m.Items;
  while items != {}
    decreases |items|
  {
    var item :| item in items;
    items := items - { item };
    print item.0, " ", item.1, "\n";
  }
}
```

 *

 * - Elements in indices [0, i) that were originally greater than k have been replaced with -1.
 * - Elements in indices [0, i) that were originally less than k retain their original values.
 * - Elements in indices [i, arr.Length) remain unchanged from their original values.
 *
 * Functional Behavior:
 * - Iterates through every element of the array from index 0 to arr.Length - 1.

 *   then arr[j] retains its original value after the method completes.
 * - The length of the array is unchanged.
 * - No elements are added or removed; only values strictly greater than k are overwritten with -1.
 *
 * @param arr  The integer array to be modified in-place.

 */

```
/**
 * Modifies an integer array in-place by replacing elements based on their relationship to a threshold value k.
 *
 * Preconditions (asserted via loop invariants):
 * - The array index i remains within bounds: 0 <= i <= arr.Length throughout execution.
 * - Elements in indices [0, i) that were originally greater than k have been replaced with -1.
 * - Elements in indices [0, i) that were originally less than k retain their original values.
 * - Elements in indices [i, arr.Length) remain unchanged from their original values.
 *
 * Functional Behavior:
 * - Iterates through every element of the array from index 0 to arr.Length - 1.
 * - For each element arr[j]:
 *   - If arr[j] > k (strictly greater than k), it is replaced with -1.
 *   - If arr[j] < k (strictly less than k), it is left unchanged.
 *   - If arr[j] == k (equal to k), it is left unchanged (no modification occurs for equal elements).
 *
 * Postconditions (deduced):
 * - For all indices j in [0, arr.Length): if the original value of arr[j] was greater than k,
 *   then arr[j] == -1 after the method completes.
 * - For all indices j in [0, arr.Length): if the original value of arr[j] was less than or equal to k,
 *   then arr[j] retains its original value after the method completes.
 * - The length of the array is unchanged.
 * - No elements are added or removed; only values strictly greater than k are overwritten with -1.
 *
 * @param arr  The integer array to be modified in-place.
 * @param k    The threshold integer value used for comparison.
 */
```


Assistant:YES

Both docstrings describe the same functional behavior. Here's why:

1. **Core operation**: Both describe replacing elements strictly greater than `k` with `-1`, while leaving elements less than or equal to `k` unchanged.

2. **In-place modification**: Both describe modifying the array in-place (the second docstring explicitly says "in-place," while the first distinguishes between `old(arr)` and `arr`, implying the same array is being modified).

3. **Threshold condition**: Both use the same condition — strictly greater than `k` (`arr[j] > k`) gets replaced with `-1`, and elements `<= k` remain unchanged. The second docstring breaks `<= k` into two cases (`< k` and `== k`) for clarity, but the net behavior is identical.

4. **Twostate specification**: The first docstring mentions using a twostate specification (distinguishing `old(arr)` from `arr`), which is consistent with the postconditions in the second docstring that reference "original values" of array elements — both are referring to the pre-state vs. post-state of the array.

5. **Scope**: Both apply the operation to all elements in the array (indices `0` to `arr.Length - 1`).

method rotate(a: array<int>, offset:int) returns (b: array<int> )

{
    assert (0<=offset);
    assert (a.Length > 0);
  b:= new int[a.Length];
  var i:=0;
  while i<a.Length
    invariant 0<=i<=a.Length
    invariant forall j ::0<=j<i ==> b[j]==a[(j+offset)%a.Length]
  {
    b[i]:=a[(i+offset)%a.Length];
    i:=i+1;
  }
}

Assistant:/**
 * Rotates an integer array by a given offset, returning a new array.
 *
 * Preconditions (asserted):
 *   - offset must be non-negative (0 <= offset).
 *   - The input array must be non-empty (a.Length > 0).
 *
 * Behavior:
 *   Given an input array `a` and a non-negative integer `offset`, this method
 *   produces a new array `b` of the same length as `a`, where each element at
 *   index `j` in `b` is assigned the value from `a` at index `(j + offset) % a.Length`.
 *   This effectively performs a left rotation of the array by `offset` positions,
 *   meaning the element that was at position `offset` in `a` becomes the first
 *   element of `b`. The rotation wraps around using modular arithmetic, so elements
 *   near the end of `a` wrap back to the beginning.
 *
 * Postconditions:
 *   - The returned array `b` has the same length as `a` (b.Length == a.Length).
 *   - For every valid index j in the range [0, a.Length), b[j] == a[(j + offset) % a.Length].
 *   - The original array `a` is not modified.
 *   - The returned array `b` is a freshly allocated array distinct from `a`.
 *
 * Example:
 *   If a = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5] and offset = 2, then b = [3, 4, 5, 1, 2].
 */

Human: Given a Dafny program. Please return an detailed docstring of the given dafny code's complete functional behavior. Do not mention implementation details. Mention 'assert' as preconditions in the docstring. Deduce postconditions. Describe every detail. Please only return the docstring. Do not explain. Below is the Dafny program:
method SelectionSort(a: array<int>)

{
  var n:= 0;
  while n != a.Length
    invariant 0 <= n <= a.Length
    invariant forall i, j :: 0 <= i < n <= j < a.Length ==> a[i] <= a[j]
    invariant forall i,j :: 0 <= i < j < n ==> a[i] <= a[j]
    invariant multiset(a[..]) == old(multiset(a[..]))
  {
    var mindex, m := n, n+1;
    while m != a.Length
      invariant n <= mindex < m <= a.Length
      invariant forall i :: n <= i < m ==> a[mindex] <= a[i]
    {
      if a[m] < a[mindex] {
        mindex := m;
      }
      m := m+1;
    }
    // Swap only if needed — but skip the swap when mindex == n
    // This subtly leaves the array unsorted in some cases
    if mindex != n {
      a[n], a[mindex] := a[mindex], a[n];
    }
    n := n+1;
  }
}

/**
 * Sorts an integer array in-place using the Selection Sort algorithm.
 *
 * Preconditions (asserted via invariants):
 *   - The array `a` is a valid, non-null array of integers.
 *
 * Postconditions / Functional Behavior:
 *   - The array `a` is sorted in non-decreasing order upon completion:
 *       forall i, j :: 0 <= i < j < a.Length ==> a[i] <= a[j]
 *   - The multiset of elements in `a` is preserved throughout execution
 *     (i.e., the method is a permutation of the original array):
 *       multiset(a[..]) == old(multiset(a[..]))
 *   - Every element in the sorted prefix is less than or equal to every
 *     element in the unsorted suffix at each step of the outer loop.
 *
 * Detailed Behavior:
 *   - The method iterates over each position `n` from index 0 to a.Length - 1
 *     (the outer loop).
 *   - For each position `n`, the inner loop scans the subarray from index `n`
 *     to a.Length - 1 to find the index `mindex` of the minimum element in
 *     that subarray.
 *   - After identifying the minimum element's index `mindex`, if `mindex`
 *     differs from `n`, the elements at positions `n` and `mindex` are
 *     swapped, placing the minimum element of the unsorted suffix at position
 *     `n`.
 *   - If `mindex == n`, no swap is performed (the minimum is already in
 *     place).
 *   - After each iteration of the outer loop, the prefix a[0..n] is fully
 *     sorted and contains the `n` smallest elements of the original array.
 *   - Loop invariants maintained by the outer loop:
 *       (1) 0 <= n <= a.Length
 *       (2) Every element in a[0..n) is <= every element in a[n..a.Length)
 *       (3) a[0..n) is sorted in non-decreasing order
 *       (4) The multiset of a[..] equals the original multiset of a[..]
 *   - Loop invariants maintained by the inner loop:
 *       (1) n <= mindex < m <= a.Length
 *       (2) a[mindex] is the minimum of a[n..m)
 *   - Upon termination of the outer loop (n == a.Length), the entire array
 *     is sorted in non-decreasing order.
 */
```

Assistant:method SetToSeq<T>(s: set<T>) returns (xs: seq<T>)
{
  xs := [];
  var remaining := s;
  while remaining != {}
    decreases |remaining|
    invariant forall x :: x in xs <==> x in s && x !in remaining
    invariant |xs| + |remaining| == |s|
  {
    var x :| x in remaining;
    xs := xs + [x];
    remaining := remaining - {x};
  }
}

method SetToSeq<T>(s: set<T>) returns (xs: seq<T>)

{
  xs := [];
  var left: set<T> := s;
  while left != {}
    invariant multiset(left) + multiset(xs) == multiset(s)
  {
    var x :| x in left;
    left := left - {x};
    xs := xs + [x];
  }
}

Assistant:/**
 * Converts a set of elements of type T into a sequence containing exactly the same elements.
 *
 * Parameters:
 *   - s: The input set of elements of type T to be converted.
 *
 * Returns:
 *   - xs: A sequence of elements of type T.
 *
 * Postconditions:
 *   - The multiset of elements in the returned sequence xs is equal to the multiset of elements in the input set s,
 *     meaning every element in s appears in xs exactly once, and xs contains no elements not in s.
 *   - The length of the returned sequence xs equals the size of the input set s (|xs| == |s|).
 *   - Every element that appears in xs also appears in s, and every element in s appears somewhere in xs.
 *   - The sequence xs is a permutation-like flattening of s with no duplicates, since sets have no duplicates
 *     and the multiset equivalence guarantees no element is added more than once.
 *
 * Behavior:
 *   - Starts with an empty sequence and iterates over all elements of s, appending each element to the sequence
 *     until all elements of s have been processed.
 *   - The order of elements in the resulting sequence is non-deterministic, as sets are unordered and element
 *     selection during iteration is arbitrary.
 *
 * Invariant (maintained throughout execution):
 *   - At every point during execution, the multiset union of the remaining unprocessed elements (left) and
 *     the already-collected elements (xs) equals the multiset of the original set s.
 */

    invariant -1 <= n < a.Length1

  {

      return m, n;

      m := m + 1;

      n := n - 1;
    }

  n := 0;

Human: Given a Dafny program. Please return an detailed docstring of the given dafny code's complete functional behavior. Do not mention implementation details. Mention 'assert' as preconditions in the docstring. Deduce postconditions. Describe every detail. Please only return the docstring. Do not explain. Below is the Dafny program:
method Swap(X: int, Y: int) returns(x: int, y: int)

{
  x, y := X, Y;

  assert x == Y || x == X;
  assert y == X || y == Y;
}

Assistant:/**
 * Swaps two integer values.
 *
 * Takes two integer inputs X and Y and returns them assigned to output variables x and y
 * such that x receives the value of X and y receives the value of Y.
 *
 * Preconditions (asserts):
 *   - x == Y || x == X: the output variable x holds either the value of Y or X
 *   - y == X || y == Y: the output variable y holds either the value of X or Y
 *
 * Postconditions:
 *   - x == X: the returned value x is equal to the original input X
 *   - y == Y: the returned value y is equal to the original input Y
 *
 * @param X - the first integer input value
 * @param Y - the second integer input value
 * @returns x - assigned the value of X
 * @returns y - assigned the value of Y
 */

Human: Determine if two docstrings describe the same functional behavior of a dafny program. Return YES or NO, and then explain the reason.
Below is the signature of the Dafny program:
method Swap(X: int, Y: int) returns(x: int, y: int)
Below is the first docstring:
/* Swap two integers. */

Below is the second docstring:
/**
 * Swaps two integer values.
 *
 * Takes two integer inputs X and Y and returns them assigned to output variables x and y
 * such that x receives the value of X and y receives the value of Y.
 *
 * Preconditions (asserts):
 *   - x == Y || x == X: the output variable x holds either the value of Y or X
 *   - y == X || y == Y: the output variable y holds either the value of X or Y
 *
 * Postconditions:
 *   - x == X: the returned value x is equal to the original input X
 *   - y == Y: the returned value y is equal to the original input Y
 *
 * @param X - the first integer input value
 * @param Y - the second integer input value
 * @returns x - assigned the value of X
 * @returns y - assigned the value of Y
 */


Assistant:NO

**Reason:**

While both docstrings describe a "swap" operation on two integers, they differ fundamentally in what the actual functional behavior is:

1. **First docstring** says "Swap two integers," which implies that the values are exchanged — i.e., `x` receives the value of `Y` and `y` receives the value of `X`. This is the standard meaning of "swap."

2. **Second docstring** explicitly states in its postconditions:
   - `x == X` (x gets the value of X)
   - `y == Y` (y gets the value of Y)

   This means the outputs are assigned the **same** values as the inputs — there is **no swap**. The values are simply passed through unchanged.

The second docstring describes a **pass-through/identity** operation (x = X, y = Y), while the first describes a true **swap** (x = Y, y = X). These are opposite behaviors, making them functionally different. The name "Swap" in the second docstring is misleading given its described postconditions.

method Triple (x:int) returns (r:int)

{
  if {
    case x<18 =>
      var a,b := 2*x, 4*x;
      r:=(a+b)/2;
    case 0<=x =>
      var y:=2*x;
      r:= x+y+1;
  }
}

Assistant:/**
 * Computes a value based on the integer input `x` using a non-deterministic conditional selection.
 *
 * The method has two cases that may be selected when their guards are satisfied:
 *
 * - **Case 1** (guard: `x < 18`):
 *   Computes `a = 2 * x` and `b = 4 * x`, then returns `r = (a + b) / 2 = (2x + 4x) / 2 = 3 * x`.
 *   The result is exactly `3 * x` (integer division of `6 * x` by 2).
 *
 * - **Case 2** (guard: `0 <= x`):
 *   Computes `y = 2 * x`, then returns `r = x + y + 1 = x + 2 * x + 1 = 3 * x + 1`.
 *   The result is `3 * x + 1`.
 *
 * **Applicability of cases:**
 * - If `x < 0`: only Case 1 is applicable, so `r = 3 * x`.
 * - If `0 <= x < 18`: both cases are applicable, so the result is non-deterministically either `3 * x` or `3 * x + 1`.
 * - If `x >= 18`: only Case 2 is applicable, so `r = 3 * x + 1`.
 * - If `x < 0` and `x >= 18` simultaneously: impossible (no valid input satisfies this).
 *
 * **Preconditions (implicit assertions):**
 * - At least one guard must be true for the method to execute without error.
 *   Specifically, the input `x` must satisfy `x < 18` or `0 <= x` (or both).
 *   This means `x` can be any integer, since for any integer either `x < 18` or `x >= 0` holds.
 *   Thus no runtime failure is possible for any integer input.
 *
 * **Postconditions (deduced):**
 * - If `x < 0`, then `r == 3 * x`.
 * - If `x >= 18`, then `r == 3 * x + 1`.
 * - If `0 <= x < 18`, then `r == 3 * x` or `r == 3 * x + 1` (non-deterministic choice).
 * - In all cases, `r == 3 * x` or `r == 3 * x + 1`.
 *
 * @param x  Any integer value.
 * @returns  `r`, which equals either `3 * x` or `3 * x + 1` depending on which guard is selected.
 */

 *   The result is exactly `3 * x` (integer division of `6 * x` by 2).

 *   Computes `y = 2 * x`, then returns `r = x + y + 1 = x + 2 * x + 1 = 3 * x + 1`.
 *   The result is `3 * x + 1`.

 * - If `x < 0`: only Case 1 is applicable, so `r = 3 * x`.
 * - If `0 <= x < 18`: both cases are applicable, so the result is non-deterministically either `3 * x` or `3 * x + 1`.
 * - If `x >= 18`: only Case 2 is applicable, so `r = 3 * x + 1`.
 * - If `x < 0` and `x >= 18` simultaneously: impossible (no valid input satisfies this).
 *
 * **Preconditions (implicit assertions):**
 * - At least one guard must be true for the method to execute without error.
 *   Specifically, the input `x` must satisfy `x < 18` or `0 <= x` (or both).
 *   This means `x` can be any integer, since for any integer either `x < 18` or `x >= 0` holds.
 *   Thus no runtime failure is possible for any integer input.

 * - If `x >= 18`, then `r == 3 * x + 1`.

**Reason:**

1. **First docstring** states the method simply triples the input with no restrictions — i.e., for any integer `x`, the result is always exactly `r = 3 * x`. This implies a single, deterministic outcome.

2. **Second docstring** describes a non-deterministic method with two conditional cases:
   - For `x < 0`: `r = 3 * x`
   - For `x >= 18`: `r = 3 * x + 1`
   - For `0 <= x < 18`: `r` is non-deterministically either `3 * x` or `3 * x + 1`

The key differences are:
- The second docstring allows `r = 3 * x + 1` as a possible result (e.g., when `x >= 0`), which contradicts the first docstring's claim that the result is always exactly `3 * x`.
- The second docstring introduces **non-determinism**, whereas the first describes a purely deterministic tripling function.
- For `x >= 18`, the second docstring guarantees `r = 3 * x + 1`, which is never `3 * x` (unless `x = 0`, but `0 < 18`), directly contradicting the first docstring.

Therefore, the two docstrings do **not** describe the same functional behavior.

/* Given an array of integers nums and an integer target, return the first pair of indices i < j of the two numbers such that they add up to target. */
/* Require that such pair (i , j) exist in the array. */
/* Ensure for all i less than returned i and for all j, that no other i, j exists. */
/* Ensure for all j between returned i j, no other j exist. */

method twoSum(nums: array<int>, target: int) returns (i: int, j: int)

Assistant:method twoSum(nums: array<int>, target: int) returns (i: int, j: int)
{
  i := 0;
  j := 1;
  
  while i < nums.Length
    invariant 0 <= i < nums.Length
    invariant forall a, b :: 0 <= a < i && a < b < nums.Length ==> nums[a] + nums[b] != target
    decreases nums.Length - i
  {
    j := i + 1;
    while j < nums.Length
      invariant i < j <= nums.Length
      invariant forall b :: i < b < j ==> nums[i] + nums[b] != target
      decreases nums.Length - j
    {
      if nums[i] + nums[j] == target {
        return;
      }
      j := j + 1;
    }
    i := i + 1;
  }

Human: Given a Dafny program. Please return an detailed docstring of the given dafny code's complete functional behavior. Do not mention implementation details. Mention 'assert' as preconditions in the docstring. Deduce postconditions. Describe every detail. Please only return the docstring. Do not explain. Below is the Dafny program:
method twoSum(nums: array<int>, target: int) returns (i: int, j: int)

{
    assert (nums.Length > 1);
    assert (exists i,j::0 <= i < j < nums.Length &&  nums[i] + nums[j] == target);
  var n := nums.Length;
  i := 0;
  j := 1;
  while i < n - 1
    invariant 0 <= i < j <= n
    invariant forall ii, jj :: 0 <= ii < i && ii < jj < n ==> nums[ii] + nums[jj] != target
  {
    j := i + 1;
    while j < n
      invariant 0 <= i < j <= n
      invariant forall jj :: i < jj < j ==> nums[i] + nums[jj] != target
    {
      if nums[i] + nums[j] == target {
        return;
      }
      j := j + 1;
    }

    i := i + 1;
    j := i + 1;
  }
  // Fallback: find any valid pair to satisfy postcondition
  i := 0;
  j := 1;
  var found := false;
  var ii := 0;
  while ii < n - 1 && !found
    invariant 0 <= ii <= n - 1
  {
    var jj := ii + 1;
    while jj < n && !found
      invariant ii < jj <= n
    {
      if nums[ii] + nums[jj] == target {
        i := ii;
        j := jj;
        found := true;
      }
      jj := jj + 1;
    }
    ii := ii + 1;
  }
}

/**
 * Finds two distinct indices in the given integer array whose corresponding values sum to the target value.

 *     indices i and j (with i strictly less than j) such that the elements at those positions

 *     to return a valid pair of indices satisfying the sum condition.
